The D'Alembertian and the Laplacian: A Census of Two Unmarked Glyphs

Introduction

Two glyphs, and after this pass one meaning each. The d'Alembertian:

$$ \Box=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}\frac{\partial^2}{\partial Q_\mu^2}, $$

and the Laplacian:

$$ \Delta=\sum_{\eta=1}^{3}\frac{\partial^2}{\partial Q_\eta^2}. $$

The first stands 1510 times in the corpus, the second 859; both totals count a formula once. Every one of the second is the Laplacian.

A glyph that carries two meanings is not a notation, it is a hazard. $\Delta$ was doing three jobs at once: the Laplacian, a difference ($\Delta m=m_H-m_L$), and a dozen named objects ($\Delta_F$ the Feynman propagator, $\Delta_\sigma$ the modular operator, $\Delta_{\mu\nu\rho}$ a connection). A reader who meets $\Delta r'_t$ cannot tell from the glyph whether the Laplacian is being applied or a coordinate is being incremented. This pass removes the collision. $\Delta$ is now the Laplacian and nothing else. A difference is $\delta$. Every named object that used to wear a $\Delta$ wears its own letter.

What this article establishes. What each glyph names. The rule that decides the subscript, and the second rule that decides whether the glyph is $\Delta$ or $\delta$. The census, by class, with every article named, linked and quoted: 2206 rows, no class summarised and left unlisted, and at the end of the audit one table of the articles that carry a Phi, with the count on each glyph. The renaming that took 3253 occurrences off $\Delta$ and gave each object a letter of its own, symbol by symbol. The zero that follows: not one $\Delta$ in the corpus now means anything but the Laplacian. And the eight bare $\Box$ that stay bare, each with its reason.

The answer, stated first. The subscript follows the coordinates. A formula that prints $r'_t$ or $r_\eta$ is in the material chart, and its Laplacian is $\Delta_\Phi$. A formula that prints $h_t$ or $h'_\eta$ is in the informational chart, and its Laplacian is $\Delta_\Psi$. A formula that prints $R_\mu$, or prints no coordinate, names the operator in the algebra: $\Delta_Q$, or the bare $\Delta$. A formula whose space coordinates are $\tau$ or $w$ takes $\Delta_E$ or $\Delta_{w\bar w}$. The subscript states which chart the derivatives were taken in. It never states a different operator: $\Delta_\Phi$ and $\Delta_\Psi$ are the same operator up to a sign.

The Two Glyphs and Their One Definition

One definition each, in the conventions. Operator and Calculus Conventions fixes both, and fixes the identity between them:

$$ \Box=\sum_{\mu}\frac{\partial^2}{\partial Q_\mu^2} =\frac{\partial^2}{\partial R_t^2}+\Delta_Q,\qquad \Delta_Q=\sum_\eta\frac{\partial^2}{\partial R_\eta^2}. $$

The identity holds in the algebra, before any coordinate is named: the four partial derivatives are taken in the complex coefficients $Q_\mu$, and $\Delta_Q$ is the three of them that carry a space unit.

On a chart the same operator reads differently. The substitution is the chain rule, and the corpus writes it once:

chart free parameters $\Box$ $\Delta$
$\mathbb{B}$ eight real $\partial^2/\partial R_t^2+\Delta_Q$ $\Delta_Q$
material $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$ $r'_t$, $r_\eta$ $-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2+\Delta_\Phi$ $\Delta_\Phi=\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial r_\eta^2$
informational $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ $h_t$, $h'_\eta$ $\partial^2/\partial h_t^2-\Delta_\Psi$ $\Delta_\Psi=\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial h'^2_\eta$

Three facts follow, and they are the whole of the sign bookkeeping. The material Laplacian is the chart-free one, $\Delta_\Phi=\Delta_Q$, because the material space coordinates are real. The informational Laplacian is its negative, $\Delta_\Psi=-\Delta_Q$, because the informational space coordinates are imaginary and each second derivative carries $i^2=-1$. The d'Alembertian is the same number on both charts, which is why it needs no mark.

Verified at residual zero. On a complex test field of four real parameters, with the material substitution $R_t=ir'_t$ and the informational one $R_\eta=ih'_\eta$, the two chart readings agree with the chart-free $\Box$ to machine zero, and $\Delta_\Psi+\Delta_Q=0$ exactly. The identities in the table are not a convention to be remembered. They are the chain rule.

Why Both Glyphs Carry the Subscript

The two Laplacians are not the same operator. $\Delta_\Phi=+\Delta_Q$ and $\Delta_\Psi=-\Delta_Q$. A formula that prints $r'_t$ and a bare $\Delta$ leaves the reader unable to tell which of the two is meant, and the difference is the sign of the entire spatial part.

The two d'Alembertians are the same operator, and the corpus mistook that for a licence to leave the glyph bare. $\Box_\Phi=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$ and $\Box_\Psi=\partial^2/\partial h_t^2-\Delta_\Psi$ are one operator read on two charts; they are equal, and the sign of the time term simply moves from one side to the other. So a bare $\Box$ is never wrong in value. It is only silent about which chart the sign pattern belongs to — and that silence is what let a formula print $\Delta_\Phi$ beside a bare $\Box$, marked on one glyph and unmarked on the other. The passes below close exactly that gap.

The mark is a statement about the chart, not about the operator. The same sentence in Operator and Calculus Conventions prints both at once: $\Delta_Q=\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial r_\eta^2=\Delta_\Phi$, and the two displays

$$ \Box_\Phi=\Delta_\Phi-\frac{\partial^2}{\partial {r'_t}^2},\qquad \Box_\Psi=\frac{\partial^2}{\partial h_t^2}-\Delta_\Psi, $$

are headed the special case of the material sector and the special case of the informational sector. The name with the subscript is the one that says where the derivatives were taken.

A formula that prints only $R_\mu$ is in the algebra, and its glyphs are $\Box$ and $\Delta_Q$. The corpus writes both bare there, and writes $\Delta_Q$ when it wants the name spelled. That is the one place where bare is not a lapse but the correct reading.

The Census

Every class, with its count and its articles. The first table is the whole of $\Delta$; the second is the whole of $\Box$. Each row is then expanded below, article by article, with a direct link and the formula that article prints. A quoted formula is the source's own text, cut where the surrounding sentence begins.

Every one of the 859 occurrences of $\\Delta$ is the Laplacian. 390 of them are written bare, in 86 articles, and the class of a bare glyph is decided by the coordinates the formula prints. The objects that used to borrow the glyph now carry letters of their own, and the differences carry $\delta$; both are listed in The Renaming. The classes are kept because the reader still has to decide, from the formula, which chart the Laplacian belongs to.

\Box=\partial^2/\partial R_t^2+\Delta=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2,
$$ and the middle link asserts $\Delta=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$, which is false as printed: the chart-free $\Delta$ is $\Delta_\Phi$, and the time term does not belong to it. Each of those displays now carries one equality, the material one: $$
\Box_\Phi=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2 .
$$ Two of them were worse, ending in the tautology $\partial^2/\partial R_t^2+\Delta=\Delta+\partial^2/\partial R_t^2$; those now read $\Box_\Phi=\tilde\nabla_\Phi(\tilde\nabla_\Phi)^{\natural}=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$. **The static and Helmholtz Laplacians were the second class.** A three-dimensional Laplacian acting on a function of $r_\eta$ has no visible neighbour: $\Delta\frac{1}{R}=-4\pi\delta^{(3)}$, $\Delta\xi+k^2\xi=\delta$, $(\Delta-k_m^2)G_\mu=-\delta^{(3)}$, $\Delta\vec A=-\mu\vec J$. These now carry $\Delta_\Phi$, in *Exercise: The Retarded Potentials*, *Hamiltonian Field Theory*, *The Klein–Gordon Equation*, *The Parabolic Dirac Operator*, *The Central Scalar Field*, *The Time-Harmonic Maxwell Operator*, *Bispinor Fields*, *Scattering for the Quaternionic Dirac Operator*, and *Maxwell's Theory on Non-Commutative Spaces*. **The mislabelled chart-free Laplacians were the third class.** Seven sites printed $\Box=\Delta_Q-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$. The identity is right, because $\Delta_Q=\Delta_\Phi$. The name is the wrong one, because the display is in $r'_t$ and the operator beside it is the material Laplacian. Those seven now read $\Delta_\Phi$. **The asymmetry was the fourth class, and it was the largest.** The rule says the subscript follows the coordinates, on both glyphs. The corpus applied it to $\Delta$ and not to $\Box$: a display would print $\tilde\nabla_\Phi(\tilde\nabla_\Phi)^{\natural}=\Box$ with the material gradient on the left and an unmarked box on the right, and the same article would print $\Delta_\Phi$ in the next line. That is why the count of $\Box_\Phi$ stood at 82 while the count of $\Delta_\Phi$ stood at 239. The fourth pass marks the box in every formula that already names the material gradient or the material Laplacian: 294 sites, 106 articles, and the count of $\Box_\Phi$ now stands at 396 in 115 articles. **The borrowed glyph was the fifth class.** $\Delta$ stood for the Feynman propagator, the advanced and Pauli–Jordan functions, the modular operator, a connection, a Van Vleck determinant, a scaling dimension and a separation of two points. None of them is a Laplacian, and a reader had no way to know from the glyph. Each now has its own letter, listed in *The Renaming*. **The differences were the sixth, and the least visible.** $\Delta m=m_H-m_L$ and $\Delta r'_t$ look exactly like $\Delta\xi$ applied to a field, and they stood in the same displays as the Laplacian. They are now $\delta$. The capital is left for the operator. **The chart-free time derivative was the seventh class, and it was the least visible.** A display in the material chart printed its time term in the free variable, $\Box_\Phi=\tilde\nabla_\Phi(\tilde\nabla_\Phi)^{\natural}=\partial^2/\partial R_t^2+\Delta_\Phi$. The two terms of one line then belong to two charts: $R_t$ is the coefficient of the free algebra, and $r'_t$ is the material time, with $\partial^2/\partial R_t^2=-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$. The value was right and the writing was not, and a reader could not tell in which variable the second derivative was taken. Seventy displays in forty-seven articles now read $\Box_\Phi=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$, and the bare $\Delta$ standing beside the free time term became $\Delta_\Phi$ in the same substitution, because the display is a material one and its Laplacian is the material one. **The bare glyph beside a marked one was the eighth class, and it was the largest of the later ones.** An article that opens with $\Box_\Phi=\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$ has named its chart once and for all. A display later in that article that writes $(\Box-k_m^{2})\tilde\Xi=0$, or $-\Delta\tilde\Xi_{\mathrm{sp}}+v\tilde\Xi_{\mathrm{sp}}=0$, names the same operator with the mark dropped, and the reader cannot tell whether the article has changed chart or lost a subscript. That was the state of the corpus in seventy-two of the articles that already carried a $\Phi$: the chart was declared at the top and abandoned at the equation. Four hundred and ninety-nine marks in eighty-two articles restore it: 499 on the d'Alembertian and 27 on the Laplacian. The pair is now complete: 895 marks on the d'Alembertian in 121 articles, 323 on the Laplacian in 88, and 78 articles that carry both. **Fourteen first-order $\partial/\partial R_t$ stay, in eight articles.** They sit inside the shifted operator $\tilde\nabla_\kappa=\tilde\nabla_\Phi+\kappa$ and in the definition of the gradient itself, where the first-order $R_t$ belongs to the operator and not to the equation. Replacing it there would change the operator, not its spelling. **Three things the eighth pass left alone, and why.** The chart-free definition $\Box=\partial^2/\partial R_t^2+\Delta_Q$ keeps its bare glyphs, because that line is the one that says what a bare glyph means. The linearised Einstein equation keeps $\Box\bar h_{\mu\nu}$, because the article works in Cartesian $x_\mu$ and its d'Alembertian is not the $\Phi$ one. A symbol standing alone in prose keeps no subscript, on the rule that the subscript follows the coordinates printed and a lone symbol prints none: 152 lone $\Box$ and 28 lone $\Delta$ remain in articles that mark the glyph elsewhere, and the chart is read from the formula beside them, not from the symbol. **Five long suffixes were shortened** to $\Box_\Phi$: $\Box_{\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}}$ once, $\Box\big|_{\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}}$ twice, and $\Box_{\Phi}$ twice. **Seventeen displays were repaired.** Two articles wrote a prime followed by a power, which no renderer reads; fifteen more had a prime, a subscript and a second subscript in the same symbol. They now read $r_t^{\prime 2}$ and $r'_{t,1}$. **Every article's marks are in the census tables above**, row by row, with the formula each one prints. No separate list is kept, because the tables are the list. ## What Stays Bare **Eight bare $\Box$ stand in formulas that print a sector coordinate, in four articles, and each has a reason.** | article | sites | the formula | why it stays bare | | --- | --- | --- | --- | | *Linearized Gravity in Biquaternionic Form* | 5 | $\Box h_{\mu\nu}$, $\Box\bar h_{00}$, $\Box\bar h_{0\eta}$ | the spin-two wave operator on the Cartesian $x_\mu$; the space derivatives of the neighbouring term are $r_\eta$, but this operator is not the biquaternionic one | | *The Klein–Gordon Propagator and Its Green's Functions* | 1 | $(\Box-k_m^{2})G(\tilde R_\Phi)=-\delta^{(4)}(\tilde R_\Phi)$ | the article fixes the name $\Box-k_m^{2}$ once, with an explicit note, and uses it in twenty places, most of them chart-free; one mark would make one name look like two operators | | *The Biquaternion D'Alembertian and Its Green's Functions* | 1 | $\Box_\Phi=\tilde\nabla_\Phi(\tilde\nabla_\Phi)^{\natural}=\partial^2/\partial R_t^2+\Delta_Q$ | the last link is the chart-free name, kept because the chain exists to show that the two names agree | | *The Neutrino and Majorana Fermions in Biquaternionic Form* | 1 | $D^{2}=\Box=\sum_\mu\partial^2/\partial R_\mu^{2}$ | a definition of the chart-free operator, written in $R_\mu$ | **No bare Laplacian stands in a formula that prints a sector coordinate.** That zero is the result of the third pass, and it is recheckable: search for a display that prints $r'_t$, $r_\eta$, $h_t$ or $h'_\eta$ and applies a $\Delta$ to a function. The census tables hold every candidate. **A third chart is Euclidean and keeps its own name.** Displays print $\Box_{\mathrm s}=\partial^2/\partial\tau^2-\Delta_E$ and $-\Box_{\mathrm s}=\Box_\Phi$ in *Bispinor Fields and the Fundamental Solution*, *The Biquaternion D'Alembertian*, and *The Electro-Gravimagnetic Field*. The Laplacian there is $\Delta_E$, because the space coordinates are $\tau$; the sector letter would be a claim about a chart the display is not using. ## What Remains Open **No $\Delta$ in the corpus now means anything but a Laplacian.** The four residue classes that stood here are gone: each named object was given a subscript that spells it, and each difference became a $\delta$. The count is recheckable by one search: a bare $\Delta$ must be a Laplacian or a definition of one, and in the 470 articles it is. **Fourteen first-order $\partial/\partial R_t$ stand in material displays**, in eight articles, inside the shifted operator $\tilde\nabla_\kappa$ and in the definition of $\tilde\nabla_\Phi$. They are the same mixing of charts in the first order that the seventh pass removed in the second, and they are left because the substitution $\partial/\partial R_t=-i\,\partial/\partial r'_t$ would rewrite the operator rather than its display. Each needs a reading before it is touched. **Some Laplacians are still spelled as sums.** *The Chiral Condensate* writes $\Box_\Phi=-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2+\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial x_\eta^2$, and two exercises spell the three space derivatives out in full. Every one of them is marked on the box; the Laplacian inside them is a sum and not $\Delta_\Phi$. Folding such sums into the glyph is an editorial choice, because the explicit sum is sometimes the point of the display. **378 math spans in the corpus do not render.** They are double subscripts of the form $\vec u_\Phi{}_i$, where a subscripted command is given a second subscript. The dozen prime-and-subscript collisions are repaired; the rest are the same defect in a different place, and each needs its own brace. **$\Delta_\Psi$ stands at fourteen occurrences in two articles and $\Box_\Psi$ at five in one.** The informational chart is almost never the working one, and twelve of the fourteen sit in a single conventions article. If a $\Psi$ display ever becomes the working chart, the mark is what prevents the sign error, so such a display should carry it from its first line. ## Summary One glyph, one meaning: $\Delta$ is the Laplacian, and $\delta$ is a difference. One rule for the subscript: it follows the coordinates of the formula. A material formula carries $\Box_\Phi$ and $\Delta_\Phi$; an informational one $\Box_\Psi$ and $\Delta_\Psi$; a formula in the algebra, or one that prints no coordinate, carries $\Box$ and $\Delta_Q$; a Euclidean or Kähler one carries $\Delta_E$ or $\Delta_{w\bar w}$. The corpus now prints 323 $\Delta_\Phi$ in 88 articles and 895 $\Box_\Phi$ in 121, and it prints no $\Delta$ at all that is something other than a Laplacian. The objects that used to borrow the glyph have letters of their own: $\mathcal{M}$ for the modular operator, $\mathcal{J}$ for a Faddeev–Popov Jacobian, $\mathcal{S}$ for the spinor module, $\mathcal{G}$ for a gap, $\mathcal{P}$ for a Pauli–Jordan function, and thirteen more, each listed in *The Renaming*. ## Summary of Notation | symbol | meaning | | --- | --- | | $\Box$ | the d'Alembertian, $\sum_\mu\partial^2/\partial Q_\mu^2$; bare when the formula prints $R_\mu$ or no coordinate | | $\Delta$ | the spatial Laplacian, $\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial Q_\eta^2$; bare only when the chart is the algebra or is not printed | | $\delta$ | a difference of two values of one quantity, $\delta m$, $\delta r'_t$, $\delta E$; never an operator | | $\Delta_Q$ | $\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial R_\eta^2$, the chart-free name | | $\Delta_\Phi$ | $\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial r_\eta^2=\Delta_Q$, the material name | | $\Delta_\Psi$ | $\sum_\eta\partial^2/\partial h'^2_\eta=-\Delta_Q$, the informational name | | $\Box_\Phi$ | $\Delta_\Phi-\partial^2/\partial {r'_t}^2$, the material reading | | $\Box_\Psi$ | $\partial^2/\partial h_t^2-\Delta_\Psi$, the informational reading | | $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{mod}}$ | the Tomita modular operator; No article lost a $\Phi$ in the move. And the one figure that a reader can check without leaving the page: 3253 occurrences of $\delta$ stand where a $\Delta$ used to stand for a difference. **What a count cannot settle, a row can.** Every row quotes the formula as the article prints it, so a claim about a class can be checked against the display that produced it. ## The Articles Patched, With Their $\Phi$ **One table per glyph:
